People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. I guess… I came in because my head just doesn’t stay where it’s supposed to anymore. Like at work, I’ll be doing inventory, counting supplies, and then I lose track of what I was counting. Or I’ll go to the supply closet and just stand there like, “What did I come in here for?”

I don’t know… frustrated is more like, I get annoyed with myself. Like, “Come on, why can’t you just remember the thing?” I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there on edge exactly.

Yeah… I guess I do avoid some things, not always on purpose. Like at work, I still get the cleaning done, I’m not just leaving stuff, but I’m slower because I keep checking myself, like, did I log that room, did I miss the supply closet, did I write it down right.

Even outside work, I’ve skipped neighborhood clean-ups and some family get-togethers I used to make time for, because I don’t want to show up and then feel like I’m losing the thread of what I’m supposed to be doing.

Actually, I used to have this junk drawer at home where everything ended up—batteries, receipts, old keys—and I kept telling myself I’d organize it, but then I’d just make it worse trying to find one thing.\n\nSo… worried? Maybe a little in the moment, but mostly I just feel irritated and kind of stuck. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not sure. I mean, I move around a lot at work because that’s the job, you know, sweeping and taking trash out and all that, so it’s hard for me to tell if that counts.\n\nIf I’m sitting somewhere, I might shift around or mess with something in my hands, but I don’t really pay attention to it. Like if I’m in the break room and there’s one of those paper coffee sleeves, I’ll fold it up without thinking, but I don’t know if that’s fidgeting or just… something to do. I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because you're worried you'll struggle to remember things or keep track of what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do avoid some things, not always on purpose. Like at work, I still get the cleaning done, I’m not just leaving stuff, but I’m slower because I keep checking myself, like, did I log that room, did I miss the supply closet, did I write it down right.\n\nAnd there was talk before about maybe trying for a supervisor kind of track, but I stopped pushing for that. It just feels like too much to keep track of, all the notes and people asking where things are. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. I mean, not like everyone suddenly hates me or anything, but I’ve pulled back some.

At work I still do the cleaning, but I’m slower, and I keep second-guessing my logs—like whether I wrote down the right room or checked the right thing. And then if someone asks me something later, I feel embarrassed, like I should know. There was even talk before about maybe moving toward a supervisor kind of thing, but I kind of stopped pushing for it. It just feels like too much to keep straight.

With family and other people… I’ve skipped some get-togethers, and those neighborhood clean-up things I used to go to. Which is kind of funny because I already clean all day, so you’d think I’d avoid cleaning on purpose, but I used to like those because you’d see people, talk a little. Now I just think, what if I forget what I’m supposed to be doing or lose track, and then I just don’t go. So yeah, I guess it’s made me more distant.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. I mean, it’s not only when I forget something, but that definitely makes it worse.

Most days it feels like there’s this heavy, flat feeling kind of following me around. Like I’ll be pushing the cleaning cart down an empty hallway, and it’s quiet except the wheels squeaking, and I’ll just feel… low. Or I’ll be sitting with my family trying to act normal, listening to whatever they’re talking about, and I’m there, but I don’t really feel there.

And then if I can’t remember something or I lose focus, it sort of proves what I was already feeling, if that makes sense. Like, there it is again. So yeah, sad, down… kind of worn out by it.

Like at work, I’ll be doing inventory, counting supplies, and then I lose track of what I was counting. Or I’ll go to the supply closet and just stand there like, “What did I come in here for?”\n\nAnd I’ve been trying to study for this supervisor certification thing—well, I’ve been meaning to, because it could help me move up—but I keep reading the same line over and over and it just doesn’t stick. It’s frustrating, because I used to be better at keeping things straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. That’s kind of what finally pushed me to come in. My head just doesn’t stay on task anymore. Like I’ll be doing inventory at work, and I’m supposed to be counting supplies, but then I lose where I was and have to start over.\n\nOr I go to the supply closet and just stand there, and it’s embarrassing because sometimes someone walks by and I pretend I’m looking for something, but really I’m trying to remember why I came in there. And with the supervisor certification stuff, I’ll read the same line again and again and it’s like it won’t stick. It makes me feel… I don’t know, like my brain is just not doing its job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed by these difficulties with focusing and remembering things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… overwhelmed, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious. It’s more like I get frustrated and then I start thinking about how I should’ve written things down, or I’ll remember this one time I lost a whole set of keys at work and spent half the shift retracing my steps.\n\nSo, yeah, it bothers me. I don’t know exactly what you mean by anxious, though.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by feeling frustrated, is it related to feeling worried or on edge about your situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… frustrated is more like, I get annoyed with myself. Like, “Come on, why can’t you just remember the thing?” I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there on edge exactly.\n\nIt’s like when you put something down and then you’re late because you can’t find it, and suddenly you’re tearing through drawers and checking the same pocket three times. Actually, I used to have this junk drawer at home where everything ended up—batteries, receipts, old keys—and I kept telling myself I’d organize it, but then I’d just make it worse trying to find one thing.\n\nSo… worried? Maybe a little in the moment, but mostly I just feel irritated and kind of stuck. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not sure. I mean, I move around a lot at work because that’s the job, you know, sweeping and taking trash out and all that, so it’s hard for me to tell if that counts.\n\nIf I’m sitting somewhere, I might shift around or mess with something in my hands, but I don’t really pay attention to it. Like if I’m in the break room and there’s one of those paper coffee sleeves, I’ll fold it up without thinking, but I don’t know if that’s fidgeting or just… something to do. I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because you're worried you'll struggle to remember things or keep track of what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do avoid some things, not always on purpose. Like at work, I still get the cleaning done, I’m not just leaving stuff, but I’m slower because I keep checking myself, like, did I log that room, did I miss the supply closet, did I write it down right.\n\nAnd there was talk before about maybe trying for a supervisor kind of track, but I stopped pushing for that. It just feels like too much to keep track of, all the notes and people asking where things are. Even outside work, I’ve skipped neighborhood clean-ups and some family get-togethers I used to make time for, because I don’t want to show up and then feel like I’m losing the thread of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers since you started having these difficulties with focusing and remembering things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, not like everyone suddenly hates me or anything, but I’ve pulled back some.\n\nAt work I still do the cleaning, but I’m slower, and I keep second-guessing my logs—like whether I wrote down the right room or checked the right thing. There was even talk before about maybe moving toward a supervisor kind of thing, but I kind of stopped pushing for it. It just feels like too much to keep straight.\n\nWith family and other people… I’ve skipped some get-togethers, and those neighborhood clean-up things I used to go to. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think it was always like this. I mean, I was never perfect with paperwork or anything, but this got noticeable maybe… about three years ago.

That was around when the contractor switched us to rotating evening and overnight shifts, especially at the bigger properties. Those places are harder to keep straight anyway—different floors, different closets, people leaving notes in weird spots. And, uh, I remember thinking at first it was just because the schedule was throwing me off. Like when you’re leaving work and the sun’s coming up, it makes the whole day feel backwards.

But yeah… about three years. That’s when it really started feeling like I couldn’t keep track the same way.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because my head just doesn’t stay where it’s supposed to anymore. Like at work, I’ll be doing inventory, counting supplies, and then I lose track of what I was counting. Or I’ll go to the supply closet and just stand there like, “What did I come in here for?”\n\nAnd I’ve been trying to study for this supervisor certification thing—well, I’ve been meaning to, because it could help me move up—but I keep reading the same line over and over and it just doesn’t stick. It’s frustrating, because I used to be better at keeping things straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. That’s kind of what finally pushed me to come in. My head just doesn’t stay on task anymore. Like I’ll be doing inventory at work, and I’m supposed to be counting supplies, but then I lose where I was and have to start over.\n\nOr I go to the supply closet and just stand there, and it’s embarrassing because sometimes someone walks by and I pretend I’m looking for something, but really I’m trying to remember why I came in there. And with the supervisor certification stuff, I’ll read the same line again and again and it’s like it won’t stick. It makes me feel… I don’t know, like my brain is just not doing its job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed by these difficulties with focusing and remembering things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… overwhelmed, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious. It’s more like I get frustrated and then I start thinking about how I should’ve written things down, or I’ll remember this one time I lost a whole set of keys at work and spent half the shift retracing my steps.\n\nSo, yeah, it bothers me. I don’t know exactly what you mean by anxious, though.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by feeling frustrated, is it related to feeling worried or on edge about your situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… frustrated is more like, I get annoyed with myself. Like, “Come on, why can’t you just remember the thing?” I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there on edge exactly.\n\nIt’s like when you put something down and then you’re late because you can’t find it, and suddenly you’re tearing through drawers and checking the same pocket three times. Actually, I used to have this junk drawer at home where everything ended up—batteries, receipts, old keys—and I kept telling myself I’d organize it, but then I’d just make it worse trying to find one thing.\n\nSo… worried? Maybe a little in the moment, but mostly I just feel irritated and kind of stuck. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not sure. I mean, I move around a lot at work because that’s the job, you know, sweeping and taking trash out and all that, so it’s hard for me to tell if that counts.\n\nIf I’m sitting somewhere, I might shift around or mess with something in my hands, but I don’t really pay attention to it. Like if I’m in the break room and there’s one of those paper coffee sleeves, I’ll fold it up without thinking, but I don’t know if that’s fidgeting or just… something to do. I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because you're worried you'll struggle to remember things or keep track of what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do avoid some things, not always on purpose. Like at work, I still get the cleaning done, I’m not just leaving stuff, but I’m slower because I keep checking myself, like, did I log that room, did I miss the supply closet, did I write it down right.\n\nAnd there was talk before about maybe trying for a supervisor kind of track, but I stopped pushing for that. It just feels like too much to keep track of, all the notes and people asking where things are. Even outside work, I’ve skipped neighborhood clean-ups and some family get-togethers I used to make time for, because I don’t want to show up and then feel like I’m losing the thread of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers since you started having these difficulties with focusing and remembering things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, not like everyone suddenly hates me or anything, but I’ve pulled back some.\n\nAt work I still do the cleaning, but I’m slower, and I keep second-guessing my logs—like whether I wrote down the right room or checked the right thing. And then if someone asks me something later, I feel embarrassed, like I should know. There was even talk before about maybe moving toward a supervisor kind of thing, but I kind of stopped pushing for it. It just feels like too much to keep straight.\n\nWith family and other people… I’ve skipped some get-togethers, and those neighborhood clean-up things I used to go to. Which is kind of funny because I already clean all day, so you’d think I’d avoid cleaning on purpose, but I used to like those because you’d see people, talk a little. Now I just think, what if I forget what I’m supposed to be doing or lose track, and then I just don’t go. So yeah, I guess it’s made me more distant.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or down when you're not able to remember things or stay focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, it’s not only when I forget something, but that definitely makes it worse.\n\nMost days it feels like there’s this heavy, flat feeling kind of following me around. Like I’ll be pushing the cleaning cart down an empty hallway, and it’s quiet except the wheels squeaking, and I’ll just feel… low. Or I’ll be sitting with my family trying to act normal, listening to whatever they’re talking about, and I’m there, but I don’t really feel there.\n\nAnd then if I can’t remember something or I lose focus, it sort of proves what I was already feeling, if that makes sense. Like, there it is again. So yeah, sad, down… kind of worn out by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble focusing and remembering things, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was always like this. That’s when it really started feeling like I couldn’t keep track the same way.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had to make significant changes to your daily routine because of these memory and focus issues?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I still get the cleaning done, but I’m slower and second-guess my logs so much that I’ve stopped pushing for the supervisor track and I’ve been skipping neighborhood clean-ups and family get-togethers I used to make time for.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I guess I have. Not like I made some big written schedule or anything, but I’ve changed what I take on.

I still get the cleaning done, you know, I’m not just leaving things unfinished, but I move slower now because I keep checking myself. Like I’ll write something in the log and then stare at it, wondering if I put the right room down or if I already did that area. It eats up time.

And there was this whole thing where I might’ve tried to move toward a supervisor spot, or at least that was kind of mentioned before, but I’ve stopped pushing for that. Too many details to keep track of, too many chances to mess something up.

Outside work, too… I don’t go to the neighborhood clean-ups much anymore, even though I used to like seeing people there. And family stuff, I skip more than I used to. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or busy, but really it’s partly because I don’t want to lose track of things or feel embarrassed. So yeah, my routine’s gotten smaller, I guess.

Like, “Come on, why can’t you just remember the thing?” I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there on edge exactly.\n\nIt’s like when you put something down and then you’re late because you can’t find it, and suddenly you’re tearing through drawers and checking the same pocket three times. Actually, I used to have this junk drawer at home where everything ended up—batteries, receipts, old keys—and I kept telling myself I’d organize it, but then I’d just make it worse trying to find one thing.\n\nSo… worried? Maybe a little in the moment, but mostly I just feel irritated and kind of stuck. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not sure. I mean, I move around a lot at work because that’s the job, you know, sweeping and taking trash out and all that, so it’s hard for me to tell if that counts.\n\nIf I’m sitting somewhere, I might shift around or mess with something in my hands, but I don’t really pay attention to it. Like if I’m in the break room and there’s one of those paper coffee sleeves, I’ll fold it up without thinking, but I don’t know if that’s fidgeting or just… something to do. I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because you're worried you'll struggle to remember things or keep track of what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do avoid some things, not always on purpose. Like at work, I still get the cleaning done, I’m not just leaving stuff, but I’m slower because I keep checking myself, like, did I log that room, did I miss the supply closet, did I write it down right.\n\nAnd there was talk before about maybe trying for a supervisor kind of track, but I stopped pushing for that. It just feels like too much to keep track of, all the notes and people asking where things are. Even outside work, I’ve skipped neighborhood clean-ups and some family get-togethers I used to make time for, because I don’t want to show up and then feel like I’m losing the thread of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers since you started having these difficulties with focusing and remembering things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Like I’ll be pushing the cleaning cart down an empty hallway, and it’s quiet except the wheels squeaking, and I’ll just feel… low. Or I’ll be sitting with my family trying to act normal, listening to whatever they’re talking about, and I’m there, but I don’t really feel there.\n\nAnd then if I can’t remember something or I lose focus, it sort of proves what I was already feeling, if that makes sense. So yeah, sad, down… kind of worn out by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble focusing and remembering things, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was always like this. I mean, I was never perfect with paperwork or anything, but this got noticeable maybe… about three years ago.\n\nThat was around when the contractor switched us to rotating evening and overnight shifts, especially at the bigger properties. Those places are harder to keep straight anyway—different floors, different closets, people leaving notes in weird spots. And, uh, I remember thinking at first it was just because the schedule was throwing me off. Like when you’re leaving work and the sun’s coming up, it makes the whole day feel backwards.\n\nBut yeah… about three years. That’s when it really started feeling like I couldn’t keep track the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make significant changes to your daily routine because of these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. Not like I made some big written schedule or anything, but I’ve changed what I take on.\n\nI still get the cleaning done, you know, I’m not just leaving things unfinished, but I move slower now because I keep checking myself. Like I’ll write something in the log and then stare at it, wondering if I put the right room down or if I already did that area. It eats up time.\n\nAnd there was this whole thing where I might’ve tried to move toward a supervisor spot, or at least that was kind of mentioned before, but I’ve stopped pushing for that. Too many details to keep track of, too many chances to mess something up.\n\nOutside work, too… I don’t go to the neighborhood clean-ups much anymore, even though I used to like seeing people there. And family stuff, I skip more than I used to. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or busy, but really it’s partly because I don’t want to lose track of things or feel embarrassed. So yeah, my routine’s gotten smaller, I guess.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that these difficulties with focusing and remembering things have significantly impacted your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I still get the cleaning done, but I’m slower and second-guess my logs so much that I’ve stopped pushing for the supervisor track and I’ve been skipping neighborhood clean-ups and family get-togethers I used to make time for.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing the job. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just not showing up or leaving trash sitting there, because I do get it done.

But it’s definitely affected it. I’m slower now because I keep checking myself, especially the logs. I’ll write something down and then keep looking back like, wait, did I put the right room, did I already do that floor, did I miss a note from somebody. It’s like I don’t trust my own head.

And, uh… there was a point where people kind of talked like maybe I could move toward supervisor stuff, but I don’t really push for that anymore. Too many things to track, and I don’t want to be the guy who messes it up.

Relationships, too, I guess. Not in some big dramatic way, but I skip more things now. Neighborhood clean-ups, family get-togethers… stuff I used to make time for. Sometimes I say I’m tired, which is true enough, but part of it is I just don’t want to be there and lose track of something or feel stupid in front of people.

It makes me feel… I don’t know, like my brain is just not doing its job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed by these difficulties with focusing and remembering things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… overwhelmed, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious. It’s more like I get frustrated and then I start thinking about how I should’ve written things down, or I’ll remember this one time I lost a whole set of keys at work and spent half the shift retracing my steps.\n\nSo, yeah, it bothers me. I don’t know exactly what you mean by anxious, though.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by feeling frustrated, is it related to feeling worried or on edge about your situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… frustrated is more like, I get annoyed with myself. Like, “Come on, why can’t you just remember the thing?” I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there on edge exactly.\n\nIt’s like when you put something down and then you’re late because you can’t find it, and suddenly you’re tearing through drawers and checking the same pocket three times. Actually, I used to have this junk drawer at home where everything ended up—batteries, receipts, old keys—and I kept telling myself I’d organize it, but then I’d just make it worse trying to find one thing.\n\nSo… worried? Maybe a little in the moment, but mostly I just feel irritated and kind of stuck. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not sure. I mean, I move around a lot at work because that’s the job, you know, sweeping and taking trash out and all that, so it’s hard for me to tell if that counts.\n\nIf I’m sitting somewhere, I might shift around or mess with something in my hands, but I don’t really pay attention to it. Like if I’m in the break room and there’s one of those paper coffee sleeves, I’ll fold it up without thinking, but I don’t know if that’s fidgeting or just… something to do. I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because you're worried you'll struggle to remember things or keep track of what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do avoid some things, not always on purpose. Like at work, I still get the cleaning done, I’m not just leaving stuff, but I’m slower because I keep checking myself, like, did I log that room, did I miss the supply closet, did I write it down right.\n\nAnd there was talk before about maybe trying for a supervisor kind of track, but I stopped pushing for that. It just feels like too much to keep track of, all the notes and people asking where things are. Even outside work, I’ve skipped neighborhood clean-ups and some family get-togethers I used to make time for, because I don’t want to show up and then feel like I’m losing the thread of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers since you started having these difficulties with focusing and remembering things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Like I’ll write something in the log and then stare at it, wondering if I put the right room down or if I already did that area. It eats up time.\n\nAnd there was this whole thing where I might’ve tried to move toward a supervisor spot, or at least that was kind of mentioned before, but I’ve stopped pushing for that. Too many details to keep track of, too many chances to mess something up.\n\nOutside work, too… I don’t go to the neighborhood clean-ups much anymore, even though I used to like seeing people there. And family stuff, I skip more than I used to. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or busy, but really it’s partly because I don’t want to lose track of things or feel embarrassed. So yeah, my routine’s gotten smaller, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these difficulties with focusing and remembering things have significantly impacted your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing the job. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just not showing up or leaving trash sitting there, because I do get it done.\n\nBut it’s definitely affected it. I’m slower now because I keep checking myself, especially the logs. I’ll write something down and then keep looking back like, wait, did I put the right room, did I already do that floor, did I miss a note from somebody. It’s like I don’t trust my own head.\n\nAnd, uh… there was a point where people kind of talked like maybe I could move toward supervisor stuff, but I don’t really push for that anymore. Too many things to track, and I don’t want to be the guy who messes it up.\n\nRelationships, too, I guess. Not in some big dramatic way, but I skip more things now. Neighborhood clean-ups, family get-togethers… stuff I used to make time for. Sometimes I say I’m tired, which is true enough, but part of it is I just don’t want to be there and lose track of something or feel stupid in front of people.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you experience these difficulties with focusing and remembering things every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Pretty much every day, yeah. Some days it’s not as bad, but it’s there.

Like I’ll be doing inventory and I’ll walk to the supply closet and then just stand there thinking, wait, what did I come in here for? Or I’ll try to study for that supervisor certification thing, and I keep reading the same line over and over, and it just doesn’t stick. Then I get annoyed and start straightening papers or checking my phone or thinking about some random thing, like whether we’re low on floor pads, and then I’ve lost another half hour.

So… yeah. It’s not every single minute, but it comes up most days.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because my head just doesn’t stay where it’s supposed to anymore. Like at work, I’ll be doing inventory, counting supplies, and then I lose track of what I was counting. Or I’ll go to the supply closet and just stand there like, “What did I come in here for?”\n\nAnd I’ve been trying to study for this supervisor certification thing—well, I’ve been meaning to, because it could help me move up—but I keep reading the same line over and over and it just doesn’t stick. It’s frustrating, because I used to be better at keeping things straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. That’s kind of what finally pushed me to come in. My head just doesn’t stay on task anymore. Like I’ll be doing inventory at work, and I’m supposed to be counting supplies, but then I lose where I was and have to start over.\n\nOr I go to the supply closet and just stand there, and it’s embarrassing because sometimes someone walks by and I pretend I’m looking for something, but really I’m trying to remember why I came in there. And with the supervisor certification stuff, I’ll read the same line again and again and it’s like it won’t stick. It makes me feel… I don’t know, like my brain is just not doing its job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed by these difficulties with focusing and remembering things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… overwhelmed, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious. It’s more like I get frustrated and then I start thinking about how I should’ve written things down, or I’ll remember this one time I lost a whole set of keys at work and spent half the shift retracing my steps.\n\nSo, yeah, it bothers me. I don’t know exactly what you mean by anxious, though.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by feeling frustrated, is it related to feeling worried or on edge about your situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… frustrated is more like, I get annoyed with myself. Like, “Come on, why can’t you just remember the thing?” I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there on edge exactly.\n\nIt’s like when you put something down and then you’re late because you can’t find it, and suddenly you’re tearing through drawers and checking the same pocket three times. Actually, I used to have this junk drawer at home where everything ended up—batteries, receipts, old keys—and I kept telling myself I’d organize it, but then I’d just make it worse trying to find one thing.\n\nSo… worried? Maybe a little in the moment, but mostly I just feel irritated and kind of stuck. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not sure. I mean, I move around a lot at work because that’s the job, you know, sweeping and taking trash out and all that, so it’s hard for me to tell if that counts.\n\nIf I’m sitting somewhere, I might shift around or mess with something in my hands, but I don’t really pay attention to it. Like if I’m in the break room and there’s one of those paper coffee sleeves, I’ll fold it up without thinking, but I don’t know if that’s fidgeting or just… something to do. I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because you're worried you'll struggle to remember things or keep track of what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do avoid some things, not always on purpose. Like at work, I still get the cleaning done, I’m not just leaving stuff, but I’m slower because I keep checking myself, like, did I log that room, did I miss the supply closet, did I write it down right.\n\nAnd there was talk before about maybe trying for a supervisor kind of track, but I stopped pushing for that. It just feels like too much to keep track of, all the notes and people asking where things are. Even outside work, I’ve skipped neighborhood clean-ups and some family get-togethers I used to make time for, because I don’t want to show up and then feel like I’m losing the thread of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers since you started having these difficulties with focusing and remembering things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, not like everyone suddenly hates me or anything, but I’ve pulled back some.\n\nAt work I still do the cleaning, but I’m slower, and I keep second-guessing my logs—like whether I wrote down the right room or checked the right thing. And then if someone asks me something later, I feel embarrassed, like I should know. There was even talk before about maybe moving toward a supervisor kind of thing, but I kind of stopped pushing for it. It just feels like too much to keep straight.\n\nWith family and other people… I’ve skipped some get-togethers, and those neighborhood clean-up things I used to go to. Which is kind of funny because I already clean all day, so you’d think I’d avoid cleaning on purpose, but I used to like those because you’d see people, talk a little. Now I just think, what if I forget what I’m supposed to be doing or lose track, and then I just don’t go. So yeah, I guess it’s made me more distant.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or down when you're not able to remember things or stay focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, it’s not only when I forget something, but that definitely makes it worse.\n\nMost days it feels like there’s this heavy, flat feeling kind of following me around. Like I’ll be pushing the cleaning cart down an empty hallway, and it’s quiet except the wheels squeaking, and I’ll just feel… low. Or I’ll be sitting with my family trying to act normal, listening to whatever they’re talking about, and I’m there, but I don’t really feel there.\n\nAnd then if I can’t remember something or I lose focus, it sort of proves what I was already feeling, if that makes sense. Like, there it is again. So yeah, sad, down… kind of worn out by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble focusing and remembering things, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was always like this. Yeah… I do. Maybe not like I’m sitting there saying “I’m worthless” all day, but it kind of comes out that way in my head.

It makes me feel… I don’t know, like my brain is just not doing its job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed by these difficulties with focusing and remembering things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… overwhelmed, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious. It’s more like I get frustrated and then I start thinking about how I should’ve written things down, or I’ll remember this one time I lost a whole set of keys at work and spent half the shift retracing my steps.\n\nSo, yeah, it bothers me. I don’t know exactly what you mean by anxious, though.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by feeling frustrated, is it related to feeling worried or on edge about your situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… frustrated is more like, I get annoyed with myself. Like, “Come on, why can’t you just remember the thing?” I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there on edge exactly.\n\nIt’s like when you put something down and then you’re late because you can’t find it, and suddenly you’re tearing through drawers and checking the same pocket three times. Actually, I used to have this junk drawer at home where everything ended up—batteries, receipts, old keys—and I kept telling myself I’d organize it, but then I’d just make it worse trying to find one thing.\n\nSo… worried? Maybe a little in the moment, but mostly I just feel irritated and kind of stuck. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not sure. I mean, I move around a lot at work because that’s the job, you know, sweeping and taking trash out and all that, so it’s hard for me to tell if that counts.\n\nIf I’m sitting somewhere, I might shift around or mess with something in my hands, but I don’t really pay attention to it. Like if I’m in the break room and there’s one of those paper coffee sleeves, I’ll fold it up without thinking, but I don’t know if that’s fidgeting or just… something to do. I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because you're worried you'll struggle to remember things or keep track of what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do avoid some things, not always on purpose. Like at work, I still get the cleaning done, I’m not just leaving stuff, but I’m slower because I keep checking myself, like, did I log that room, did I miss the supply closet, did I write it down right.\n\nAnd there was talk before about maybe trying for a supervisor kind of track, but I stopped pushing for that. It just feels like too much to keep track of, all the notes and people asking where things are. Even outside work, I’ve skipped neighborhood clean-ups and some family get-togethers I used to make time for, because I don’t want to show up and then feel like I’m losing the thread of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers since you started having these difficulties with focusing and remembering things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, not like everyone suddenly hates me or anything, but I’ve pulled back some.\n\nAt work I still do the cleaning, but I’m slower, and I keep second-guessing my logs—like whether I wrote down the right room or checked the right thing. And then if someone asks me something later, I feel embarrassed, like I should know. There was even talk before about maybe moving toward a supervisor kind of thing, but I kind of stopped pushing for it. It just feels like too much to keep straight.\n\nWith family and other people… I’ve skipped some get-togethers, and those neighborhood clean-up things I used to go to. Which is kind of funny because I already clean all day, so you’d think I’d avoid cleaning on purpose, but I used to like those because you’d see people, talk a little. Now I just think, what if I forget what I’m supposed to be doing or lose track, and then I just don’t go. So yeah, I guess it’s made me more distant.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or down when you're not able to remember things or stay focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, it’s not only when I forget something, but that definitely makes it worse.\n\nMost days it feels like there’s this heavy, flat feeling kind of following me around. Like I’ll be pushing the cleaning cart down an empty hallway, and it’s quiet except the wheels squeaking, and I’ll just feel… low. Or I’ll be sitting with my family trying to act normal, listening to whatever they’re talking about, and I’m there, but I don’t really feel there.\n\nAnd then if I can’t remember something or I lose focus, it sort of proves what I was already feeling, if that makes sense. Like, there it is again. So yeah, sad, down… kind of worn out by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble focusing and remembering things, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was always like this. I mean, I was never perfect with paperwork or anything, but this got noticeable maybe… about three years ago.\n\nThat was around when the contractor switched us to rotating evening and overnight shifts, especially at the bigger properties. Those places are harder to keep straight anyway—different floors, different closets, people leaving notes in weird spots. And, uh, I remember thinking at first it was just because the schedule was throwing me off. Like when you’re leaving work and the sun’s coming up, it makes the whole day feel backwards.\n\nBut yeah… about three years. That’s when it really started feeling like I couldn’t keep track the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make significant changes to your daily routine because of these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. Not like I made some big written schedule or anything, but I’ve changed what I take on.\n\nI still get the cleaning done, you know, I’m not just leaving things unfinished, but I move slower now because I keep checking myself. Like I’ll write something in the log and then stare at it, wondering if I put the right room down or if I already did that area. It eats up time.\n\nAnd there was this whole thing where I might’ve tried to move toward a supervisor spot, or at least that was kind of mentioned before, but I’ve stopped pushing for that. Too many details to keep track of, too many chances to mess something up.\n\nOutside work, too… I don’t go to the neighborhood clean-ups much anymore, even though I used to like seeing people there. And family stuff, I skip more than I used to. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or busy, but really it’s partly because I don’t want to lose track of things or feel embarrassed. So yeah, my routine’s gotten smaller, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these difficulties with focusing and remembering things have significantly impacted your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing the job. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just not showing up or leaving trash sitting there, because I do get it done.\n\nBut it’s definitely affected it. I’m slower now because I keep checking myself, especially the logs. I’ll write something down and then keep looking back like, wait, did I put the right room, did I already do that floor, did I miss a note from somebody. It’s like I don’t trust my own head.\n\nAnd, uh… there was a point where people kind of talked like maybe I could move toward supervisor stuff, but I don’t really push for that anymore. Too many things to track, and I don’t want to be the guy who messes it up.\n\nRelationships, too, I guess. Not in some big dramatic way, but I skip more things now. Neighborhood clean-ups, family get-togethers… stuff I used to make time for. Sometimes I say I’m tired, which is true enough, but part of it is I just don’t want to be there and lose track of something or feel stupid in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focusing and remembering things every day?\n\nPatient: Pretty much every day, yeah. Some days it’s not as bad, but it’s there.\n\nLike I’ll be doing inventory and I’ll walk to the supply closet and then just stand there thinking, wait, what did I come in here for? Or I’ll try to study for that supervisor certification thing, and I keep reading the same line over and over, and it just doesn’t stick. Then I get annoyed and start straightening papers or checking my phone or thinking about some random thing, like whether we’re low on floor pads, and then I’ve lost another half hour.\n\nSo… yeah. It’s not every single minute, but it comes up most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about your situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Maybe not like I’m sitting there saying “I’m worthless” all day, but it kind of comes out that way in my head.\n\nI’ll think, you know, other guys my age would’ve figured this out by now. They’d adjust, move up, take on more. And I’m still over here trying not to miss small things at work, like checking the same notes again or worrying I messed up some little detail.\n\nIt makes me feel like… maybe I’m just not cut out for more than this. And then I feel bad even saying that, because the job matters, it’s honest work. My uncle used to do maintenance at a school, actually, and he was proud of it—he had this whole thing about keeping floors looking good—but, I don’t know, for me it turns into this thing where I feel like I should be doing better by now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I… I don’t know. I mean, I get really low sometimes, but “better off dead” is a big thing to say.

So yeah, sad, down… kind of worn out by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble focusing and remembering things, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was always like this. Those places are harder to keep straight anyway—different floors, different closets, people leaving notes in weird spots. And, uh, I remember thinking at first it was just because the schedule was throwing me off. Like when you’re leaving work and the sun’s coming up, it makes the whole day feel backwards.\n\nBut yeah… about three years. That’s when it really started feeling like I couldn’t keep track the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make significant changes to your daily routine because of these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. Not like I made some big written schedule or anything, but I’ve changed what I take on.\n\nI still get the cleaning done, you know, I’m not just leaving things unfinished, but I move slower now because I keep checking myself. Like I’ll write something in the log and then stare at it, wondering if I put the right room down or if I already did that area. It eats up time.\n\nAnd there was this whole thing where I might’ve tried to move toward a supervisor spot, or at least that was kind of mentioned before, but I’ve stopped pushing for that. Too many details to keep track of, too many chances to mess something up.\n\nOutside work, too… I don’t go to the neighborhood clean-ups much anymore, even though I used to like seeing people there. And family stuff, I skip more than I used to. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or busy, but really it’s partly because I don’t want to lose track of things or feel embarrassed. So yeah, my routine’s gotten smaller, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these difficulties with focusing and remembering things have significantly impacted your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing the job. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just not showing up or leaving trash sitting there, because I do get it done.\n\nBut it’s definitely affected it. I’m slower now because I keep checking myself, especially the logs. I’ll write something down and then keep looking back like, wait, did I put the right room, did I already do that floor, did I miss a note from somebody. It’s like I don’t trust my own head.\n\nAnd, uh… there was a point where people kind of talked like maybe I could move toward supervisor stuff, but I don’t really push for that anymore. Too many things to track, and I don’t want to be the guy who messes it up.\n\nRelationships, too, I guess. Not in some big dramatic way, but I skip more things now. Neighborhood clean-ups, family get-togethers… stuff I used to make time for. Sometimes I say I’m tired, which is true enough, but part of it is I just don’t want to be there and lose track of something or feel stupid in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focusing and remembering things every day?\n\nPatient: Pretty much every day, yeah. Some days it’s not as bad, but it’s there.\n\nLike I’ll be doing inventory and I’ll walk to the supply closet and then just stand there thinking, wait, what did I come in here for? Or I’ll try to study for that supervisor certification thing, and I keep reading the same line over and over, and it just doesn’t stick. Then I get annoyed and start straightening papers or checking my phone or thinking about some random thing, like whether we’re low on floor pads, and then I’ve lost another half hour.\n\nSo… yeah. It’s not every single minute, but it comes up most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about your situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Maybe not like I’m sitting there saying “I’m worthless” all day, but it kind of comes out that way in my head.\n\nI’ll think, you know, other guys my age would’ve figured this out by now. They’d adjust, move up, take on more. And I’m still over here trying not to miss small things at work, like checking the same notes again or worrying I messed up some little detail.\n\nIt makes me feel like… maybe I’m just not cut out for more than this. And then I feel bad even saying that, because the job matters, it’s honest work. My uncle used to do maintenance at a school, actually, and he was proud of it—he had this whole thing about keeping floors looking good—but, I don’t know, for me it turns into this thing where I feel like I should be doing better by now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down that you thought you'd be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I mean, I get really low sometimes, but “better off dead” is a big thing to say.\n\nSometimes I think more like… I wish I could just not have to deal with everything for a while, you know? Like when the trash compactor jams and then the whole loading area smells bad and everyone’s acting like it’s your fault, even though it’s usually somebody putting cardboard in wrong—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure how to answer it. I’ve felt pretty down, but I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yeah… most days, I’d say. It’s not always like I’m crying or anything obvious like that, but there’s this heavy, flat feeling that kind of follows me around.

Like I’ll be pushing the cart down an empty hallway at work, and everything’s quiet except the wheels squeaking—actually one of them’s been messed up for weeks, I keep meaning to ask about replacing it—and I’ll just feel… low. Or I’m at home with my family, sitting there trying to act normal, and it’s still there in the background.

So, yeah. For the past couple weeks, most days.

Like if I’m in the break room and there’s one of those paper coffee sleeves, I’ll fold it up without thinking, but I don’t know if that’s fidgeting or just… something to do. I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because you're worried you'll struggle to remember things or keep track of what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do avoid some things, not always on purpose. Like at work, I still get the cleaning done, I’m not just leaving stuff, but I’m slower because I keep checking myself, like, did I log that room, did I miss the supply closet, did I write it down right.\n\nAnd there was talk before about maybe trying for a supervisor kind of track, but I stopped pushing for that. It just feels like too much to keep track of, all the notes and people asking where things are. Even outside work, I’ve skipped neighborhood clean-ups and some family get-togethers I used to make time for, because I don’t want to show up and then feel like I’m losing the thread of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers since you started having these difficulties with focusing and remembering things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, not like everyone suddenly hates me or anything, but I’ve pulled back some.\n\nAt work I still do the cleaning, but I’m slower, and I keep second-guessing my logs—like whether I wrote down the right room or checked the right thing. And then if someone asks me something later, I feel embarrassed, like I should know. There was even talk before about maybe moving toward a supervisor kind of thing, but I kind of stopped pushing for it. It just feels like too much to keep straight.\n\nWith family and other people… I’ve skipped some get-togethers, and those neighborhood clean-up things I used to go to. Which is kind of funny because I already clean all day, so you’d think I’d avoid cleaning on purpose, but I used to like those because you’d see people, talk a little. Now I just think, what if I forget what I’m supposed to be doing or lose track, and then I just don’t go. So yeah, I guess it’s made me more distant.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or down when you're not able to remember things or stay focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, it’s not only when I forget something, but that definitely makes it worse.\n\nMost days it feels like there’s this heavy, flat feeling kind of following me around. Like I’ll be pushing the cleaning cart down an empty hallway, and it’s quiet except the wheels squeaking, and I’ll just feel… low. Or I’ll be sitting with my family trying to act normal, listening to whatever they’re talking about, and I’m there, but I don’t really feel there.\n\nAnd then if I can’t remember something or I lose focus, it sort of proves what I was already feeling, if that makes sense. Like, there it is again. So yeah, sad, down… kind of worn out by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble focusing and remembering things, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was always like this. I mean, I was never perfect with paperwork or anything, but this got noticeable maybe… about three years ago.\n\nThat was around when the contractor switched us to rotating evening and overnight shifts, especially at the bigger properties. Those places are harder to keep straight anyway—different floors, different closets, people leaving notes in weird spots. And, uh, I remember thinking at first it was just because the schedule was throwing me off. Like when you’re leaving work and the sun’s coming up, it makes the whole day feel backwards.\n\nBut yeah… about three years. That’s when it really started feeling like I couldn’t keep track the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make significant changes to your daily routine because of these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. Not like I made some big written schedule or anything, but I’ve changed what I take on.\n\nI still get the cleaning done, you know, I’m not just leaving things unfinished, but I move slower now because I keep checking myself. Like I’ll write something in the log and then stare at it, wondering if I put the right room down or if I already did that area. It eats up time.\n\nAnd there was this whole thing where I might’ve tried to move toward a supervisor spot, or at least that was kind of mentioned before, but I’ve stopped pushing for that. Too many details to keep track of, too many chances to mess something up.\n\nOutside work, too… I don’t go to the neighborhood clean-ups much anymore, even though I used to like seeing people there. And family stuff, I skip more than I used to. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or busy, but really it’s partly because I don’t want to lose track of things or feel embarrassed. So yeah, my routine’s gotten smaller, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these difficulties with focusing and remembering things have significantly impacted your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing the job. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just not showing up or leaving trash sitting there, because I do get it done.\n\nBut it’s definitely affected it. I’m slower now because I keep checking myself, especially the logs. I’ll write something down and then keep looking back like, wait, did I put the right room, did I already do that floor, did I miss a note from somebody. It’s like I don’t trust my own head.\n\nAnd, uh… there was a point where people kind of talked like maybe I could move toward supervisor stuff, but I don’t really push for that anymore. Too many things to track, and I don’t want to be the guy who messes it up.\n\nRelationships, too, I guess. Not in some big dramatic way, but I skip more things now. Neighborhood clean-ups, family get-togethers… stuff I used to make time for. My uncle used to do maintenance at a school, actually, and he was proud of it—he had this whole thing about keeping floors looking good—but, I don’t know, for me it turns into this thing where I feel like I should be doing better by now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt so down that you thought you'd be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I mean, I get really low sometimes, but “better off dead” is a big thing to say.\n\nSometimes I think more like… I wish I could just not have to deal with everything for a while, you know? Like when the trash compactor jams and then the whole loading area smells bad and everyone’s acting like it’s your fault, even though it’s usually somebody putting cardboard in wrong—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I’m not sure how to answer it. I’ve felt pretty down, but I don’t know if I’d put it exactly that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most days for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I’d say. It’s not always like I’m crying or anything obvious like that, but there’s this heavy, flat feeling that kind of follows me around.\n\nLike I’ll be pushing the cart down an empty hallway at work, and everything’s quiet except the wheels squeaking—actually one of them’s been messed up for weeks, I keep meaning to ask about replacing it—and I’ll just feel… low. Or I’m at home with my family, sitting there trying to act normal, and it’s still there in the background.\n\nSo, yeah. For the past couple weeks, most days.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

I’m not sure… I mean, I still do some things, but I don’t know if I enjoy them the same or if I’m just doing them out of habit.

Like sometimes I’ll put on a game or try to watch something, and then I realize I’ve been looking at my phone half the time anyway. But then again, maybe everybody does that now. My cousin can’t watch anything without scrolling, and he says that’s normal, so I don’t know.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because my head just doesn’t stay where it’s supposed to anymore. Like at work, I’ll be doing inventory, counting supplies, and then I lose track of what I was counting. Or I’ll go to the supply closet and just stand there like, “What did I come in here for?”\n\nAnd I’ve been trying to study for this supervisor certification thing—well, I’ve been meaning to, because it could help me move up—but I keep reading the same line over and over and it just doesn’t stick. It’s frustrating, because I used to be better at keeping things straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things you're trying to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. That’s kind of what finally pushed me to come in. My head just doesn’t stay on task anymore. Like I’ll be doing inventory at work, and I’m supposed to be counting supplies, but then I lose where I was and have to start over.\n\nOr I go to the supply closet and just stand there, and it’s embarrassing because sometimes someone walks by and I pretend I’m looking for something, but really I’m trying to remember why I came in there. And with the supervisor certification stuff, I’ll read the same line again and again and it’s like it won’t stick. It makes me feel… I don’t know, like my brain is just not doing its job.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed by these difficulties with focusing and remembering things?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… overwhelmed, maybe sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious. It’s more like I get frustrated and then I start thinking about how I should’ve written things down, or I’ll remember this one time I lost a whole set of keys at work and spent half the shift retracing my steps.\n\nSo, yeah, it bothers me. I don’t know exactly what you mean by anxious, though.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by feeling frustrated, is it related to feeling worried or on edge about your situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… frustrated is more like, I get annoyed with myself. Like, “Come on, why can’t you just remember the thing?” I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there on edge exactly.\n\nIt’s like when you put something down and then you’re late because you can’t find it, and suddenly you’re tearing through drawers and checking the same pocket three times. Actually, I used to have this junk drawer at home where everything ended up—batteries, receipts, old keys—and I kept telling myself I’d organize it, but then I’d just make it worse trying to find one thing.\n\nSo… worried? Maybe a little in the moment, but mostly I just feel irritated and kind of stuck. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Restless… I’m not sure. I mean, I move around a lot at work because that’s the job, you know, sweeping and taking trash out and all that, so it’s hard for me to tell if that counts.\n\nIf I’m sitting somewhere, I might shift around or mess with something in my hands, but I don’t really pay attention to it. Like if I’m in the break room and there’s one of those paper coffee sleeves, I’ll fold it up without thinking, but I don’t know if that’s fidgeting or just… something to do. I guess I haven’t really noticed it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain tasks or activities because you're worried you'll struggle to remember things or keep track of what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do avoid some things, not always on purpose. Like at work, I still get the cleaning done, I’m not just leaving stuff, but I’m slower because I keep checking myself, like, did I log that room, did I miss the supply closet, did I write it down right.\n\nAnd there was talk before about maybe trying for a supervisor kind of track, but I stopped pushing for that. It just feels like too much to keep track of, all the notes and people asking where things are. Even outside work, I’ve skipped neighborhood clean-ups and some family get-togethers I used to make time for, because I don’t want to show up and then feel like I’m losing the thread of what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers since you started having these difficulties with focusing and remembering things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, not like everyone suddenly hates me or anything, but I’ve pulled back some.\n\nAt work I still do the cleaning, but I’m slower, and I keep second-guessing my logs—like whether I wrote down the right room or checked the right thing. And then if someone asks me something later, I feel embarrassed, like I should know. There was even talk before about maybe moving toward a supervisor kind of thing, but I kind of stopped pushing for it. It just feels like too much to keep straight.\n\nWith family and other people… I’ve skipped some get-togethers, and those neighborhood clean-up things I used to go to. Which is kind of funny because I already clean all day, so you’d think I’d avoid cleaning on purpose, but I used to like those because you’d see people, talk a little. Now I just think, what if I forget what I’m supposed to be doing or lose track, and then I just don’t go. So yeah, I guess it’s made me more distant.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sad or down when you're not able to remember things or stay focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, it’s not only when I forget something, but that definitely makes it worse.\n\nMost days it feels like there’s this heavy, flat feeling kind of following me around. Like I’ll be pushing the cleaning cart down an empty hallway, and it’s quiet except the wheels squeaking, and I’ll just feel… low. Or I’ll be sitting with my family trying to act normal, listening to whatever they’re talking about, and I’m there, but I don’t really feel there.\n\nAnd then if I can’t remember something or I lose focus, it sort of proves what I was already feeling, if that makes sense. Like, there it is again. So yeah, sad, down… kind of worn out by it.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble focusing and remembering things, or is this a relatively new problem for you?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it was always like this. I mean, I was never perfect with paperwork or anything, but this got noticeable maybe… about three years ago.\n\nThat was around when the contractor switched us to rotating evening and overnight shifts, especially at the bigger properties. Those places are harder to keep straight anyway—different floors, different closets, people leaving notes in weird spots. And, uh, I remember thinking at first it was just because the schedule was throwing me off. Like when you’re leaving work and the sun’s coming up, it makes the whole day feel backwards.\n\nBut yeah… about three years. That’s when it really started feeling like I couldn’t keep track the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make significant changes to your daily routine because of these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. Not like I made some big written schedule or anything, but I’ve changed what I take on.\n\nI still get the cleaning done, you know, I’m not just leaving things unfinished, but I move slower now because I keep checking myself. Like I’ll write something in the log and then stare at it, wondering if I put the right room down or if I already did that area. It eats up time.\n\nAnd there was this whole thing where I might’ve tried to move toward a supervisor spot, or at least that was kind of mentioned before, but I’ve stopped pushing for that. Too many details to keep track of, too many chances to mess something up.\n\nOutside work, too… I don’t go to the neighborhood clean-ups much anymore, even though I used to like seeing people there. And family stuff, I skip more than I used to. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or busy, but really it’s partly because I don’t want to lose track of things or feel embarrassed. So yeah, my routine’s gotten smaller, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these difficulties with focusing and remembering things have significantly impacted your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing the job. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just not showing up or leaving trash sitting there, because I do get it done.\n\nBut it’s definitely affected it. I’m slower now because I keep checking myself, especially the logs. I’ll write something down and then keep looking back like, wait, did I put the right room, did I already do that floor, did I miss a note from somebody. It’s like I don’t trust my own head.\n\nAnd, uh… there was a point where people kind of talked like maybe I could move toward supervisor stuff, but I don’t really push for that anymore. Too many things to track, and I don’t want to be the guy who messes it up.\n\nRelationships, too, I guess. Not in some big dramatic way, but I skip more things now. Neighborhood clean-ups, family get-togethers… stuff I used to make time for. Sometimes I say I’m tired, which is true enough, but part of it is I just don’t want to be there and lose track of something or feel stupid in front of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focusing and remembering things every day?\n\nPatient: Pretty much every day, yeah. Some days it’s not as bad, but it’s there.\n\nLike I’ll be doing inventory and I’ll walk to the supply closet and then just stand there thinking, wait, what did I come in here for? Or I’ll try to study for that supervisor certification thing, and I keep reading the same line over and over, and it just doesn’t stick. Then I get annoyed and start straightening papers or checking my phone or thinking about some random thing, like whether we’re low on floor pads, and then I’ve lost another half hour.\n\nSo… yeah. It’s not every single minute, but it comes up most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about your situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Maybe not like I’m sitting there saying “I’m worthless” all day, but it kind of comes out that way in my head.\n\nI’ll think, you know, other guys my age would’ve figured this out by now. That’s when it really started feeling like I couldn’t keep track the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make significant changes to your daily routine because of these memory and focus issues?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I have. Not like I made some big written schedule or anything, but I’ve changed what I take on.\n\nI still get the cleaning done, you know, I’m not just leaving things unfinished, but I move slower now because I keep checking myself. Like I’ll write something in the log and then stare at it, wondering if I put the right room down or if I already did that area. It eats up time.\n\nAnd there was this whole thing where I might’ve tried to move toward a supervisor spot, or at least that was kind of mentioned before, but I’ve stopped pushing for that. Too many details to keep track of, too many chances to mess something up.\n\nOutside work, too… I don’t go to the neighborhood clean-ups much anymore, even though I used to like seeing people there. And family stuff, I skip more than I used to. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or busy, but really it’s partly because I don’t want to lose track of things or feel embarrassed. So yeah, my routine’s gotten smaller, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that these difficulties with focusing and remembering things have significantly impacted your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still doing the job. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just not showing up or leaving trash sitting there, because I do get it done.\n\nBut it’s definitely affected it. I’m slower now because I keep checking myself, especially the logs. I’ll write something down and then keep looking back like, wait, did I put the right room, did I already do that floor, did I miss a note from somebody. It’s like I don’t trust my own head.\n\nAnd, uh… there was a point where people kind of talked like maybe I could move toward supervisor stuff, but I don’t really push for that anymore. Too many things to track, and I don’t want to be the guy who messes it up.\n\nRelationships, too, I guess. Not in some big dramatic way, but I skip more things now. Neighborhood clean-ups, family get-togethers… stuff I used to make time for.
